**Exudyn revision plan 2026c** - the open work after the 1.13 release, by group. It continues
[`exudynRevisionPlan2026b.md`](exudynRevisionPlan2026b.md) (closed 2026-10-10, a record now) and
[`exudynRevisionPlan2026.md`](exudynRevisionPlan2026.md) (closed as 1.12). What was done is written up in
[`exudynRevisionLog2026c.md`](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md); facts and decisions are in
[`exudynRevisionInfo2026.md`](exudynRevisionInfo2026.md).

# Revision plan how to

**The file** has four sections, each exactly once and in this order: *how to* (these rules), *next steps*
(hand-written), *open steps* (generated) and *groups* (a generated table, then the groups and their steps).
`python docs/revision/planManager.py` checks the plan, writes the generated parts, lists, shows, adds and changes
steps. **Run `planManager.py update` after every edit**; it must end with `OK`.

**A group or step is a heading** that starts with its marker and its title:

- `## **OPEN_RGx** Title` for a group, `### **OPEN_RGx.y** Title` for a step, `#### **CLOSED_RGx.y.z** Title` for
  a sub-step - the heading level follows from the number of parts;
- the marker is `OPEN_` or `CLOSED_`, and it appears **only** in that heading, so a search for `_RGx.y**` finds
  the step and `OPEN_RG` finds every open one;
- a step reaches from its heading to the next heading;
- **the title says what is done in the step, and how** - not why, not when, not what was there before.

**Status.** A group is open as long as one of its steps is open, and closed otherwise. A closed step has no open
step below it. A closed step keeps its title and one or two lines - date, issues, outcome; the details go to the log.

**Closing a group or a significant step** includes the test suite and the performance tests with the fast module,
the performance tests with the regular module (#3299), and the AVX2 references recorded again if they moved (docs/dev/WORKFLOW.md section 8, #3296).

**Numbers are permanent.** `RGx.y.z`, given when the step is written down and never reused - also not a number that
revision2026b gave, which `planManager.py add` knows. The content of a step may change when the plan changes or a
measurement points elsewhere; its number does not. Groups RG1 to RG11 continue those of revision2026b with their
numbers; the higher groups of revision2026b are closed, and what was left of them is in RG11.

**Referring to a step**: `RG4.21` inside this plan, never with the marker; "revision2026c step RG4.21" outside it, and
"revision2026b step RG12.29" or "revision2026 step R4.3" for the closed plans. **Code, commits and documentation cite
the issue** (`#2884`), not the plan: the plan is internal.

**Issues carry the content.** A step is first written down with what is to be done. Once it has an issue, the issue
holds the description and the step keeps the issue number and a short line - plus what does not belong in an issue:
explanations, decisions of the maintainer, measurements that steer the plan.

**New groups and new steps at the level RGx.y** are proposed to the maintainer before they are written down;
sub-steps may be added directly.

**Next steps** are hand-written: the main open steps and the recommended next ones, usually with some that can be
done now without other people's time or open decisions. `planManager.py` checks that each step named there exists
and is open. **Open steps** and the **groups** table are generated - never edit them by hand.

# Revision plan next steps

The title of each says what it does; the sentence after it, why it comes now.

1. **Rehearse the next release on a `release/*` branch** (the release probably around the end of November 2026, before the removal date of #3361) with *build as a release* - the first run of cibuildwheel
   4.3.0 and of cp315 on GitHub.
2. **Cheaper reflections** (RG6.26.12): no shadow rays for the hits of weak reflections, and probe rays for blurred
   reflections; the reflections cost 40 % of an image and the blurred ones a multiple of that.
3. **The measurements of the temporaries** (RG5.2.7.1): they decide whether fused vector expressions (RG5.2.7.5) pay.
4. **The larger open work**, once the next release is planned: the plugin interface (RG8).

# Revision plan open steps

- **RG4** Implementation problems and bugs
  - RG4.20 Port the revision of ObjectANCFThinPlate and complete the element (1 open sub-step)
  - RG4.22 Add a 3D sliding joint with rotation, tested with the ANCF cable and beam
- **RG5** Performance
  - RG5.2 Make the hot linear algebra vectorizable with compile-time sizes (3 open sub-steps)
  - RG5.5 Record symbolic user functions of specific objects in Python and evaluate them on a GPU
  - RG5.10 Solve the systems of one SystemContainer in parallel, each on its own thread
- **RG6** Graphics and rendering
  - RG6.26 Raytracer: the cost of shadows and soft shadows, and the options to reduce it (1 open sub-step)
- **RG7** Python user items
  - RG7.15 Complete the user items after the maintainer's review: data and post-Newton of the connector, the Jacobian of the joint, the body marker
  - RG7.18 Add MarkerNodeUser, a marker on the coordinates of nodes by Python user functions, for the coordinate constraint as user item
- **RG8** Compiled C++ user items
  - RG8.1 Move the item registry into one exported translation unit so that plugins share it
  - RG8.2 Check an ABI fingerprint when a plugin registers, and refuse a mismatch with a message
  - RG8.3 Build a reference plugin with every build and test it in a system
  - RG8.4 Ship the plugin headers in the wheel and add exudyn.get_include()
  - RG8.5 Report duplicate item names at registration instead of aborting the import
  - RG8.6 Discover plugins at import from a directory, a variable and entry points, each loaded in isolation
  - RG8.7 Record the loaded plugins and their versions in the solver log and the solution file header
  - RG8.8 Generate a plugin skeleton from a user's definition file
  - RG8.9 Document what a plugin may and must do
  - RG8.10 Let an item take a user function compiled in C++
- **RG9** Structural core improvements
  - RG9.7 Replace the Cable2D markers by general markers for curves, surfaces and volumes
- **RG12** Symbolic
  - RG12.71 Evaluate one source for the C++ items, the symbolic user items and their documentation (3 open sub-steps)
- **RG14** Uniform evaluation of symbolic systems on the CPU and the GPU
  - RG14.2 Evaluate a symbolic system by levels of numpy ufuncs on the CPU, for one state and for ensembles (1 open sub-step)
  - RG14.3 Run the arrays of the uniform evaluation by CuPy on the GPU
  - RG14.4 Evaluate the kinds by one Numba-compiled function per kind, with explicit integration
  - RG14.5 Export the equations of motion as C++ and Python - the programs, the sparsity pattern and the scatter -, the CPU reference of the GPU tests
  - RG14.6 User items of GenericJoint, HydraulicActuatorSimple, ReevingSystemSprings and ObjectFFRFreducedOrder for the advanced GPU tests
- **RG19** Revision of the robotics submodule
  - RG19.14 A floating base for legged robots; wheeled robots keep their joints x, y and the rotation (1 open sub-step)
  - RG19.15 The joint torque loads: the test models on SetControl now, the deprecated parameters removed at the end of the deprecation period (1 open sub-step)
  - RG19.18 ProfileJointPath: no line of length zero between two blends
- **RG20** Advanced solvers
  - RG20.3 Adjoint sensitivities on the symbolic system for optimization (1 open sub-step)
- **RG22** Nonlinear finite elements: tetrahedra
  - RG22.1 Geometrically nonlinear 4- and 10-node tetrahedra
  - RG22.2 MarkerSuperElementRigid: the tangent operator in the rotation Jacobian without useAlternativeApproach

Linux and macOS ARM give the Windows results - all 193 test models and 94 mini examples within their tolerance, the
former macOS-only models with an error of 0 or 1e-16. `UnresolvedOnLinux()` is empty, `UnresolvedOnMacOS()` keeps
objectFFRFreducedOrderTest.py for cp314, whose modes come from numpy. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg4-1)

The known differences are listed in `UnresolvedOnLinux()` and `UnresolvedOnMacOS()` (`python/testing/runTestSuiteRefSol.py`,
the macOS list also for Linux ARM, #2919); every entry removed is a real fix. The measurements and their history are in
the log of revision2026b step RG4.1.

CLOSED 2026-10-16 (#2990): Eigen's fused multiply-add on NEON (`__ARM_FEATURE_FMA`, `EIGEN_HAS_SINGLE_INSTRUCTION_MADD`).

ANCF contact and sliding models and a gravity connector (`ANCFbeltDrive`, `ANCFcontactCircleTest`,
`ANCFgeneralContactCircle`, `ANCFslidingAndALEjointTest`, `connectorGravityTest`), and since 1.13
`geometricallyExactBeamRightAngleFrame` and `objectFFRFreducedOrderTest` (cp314). `-ffp-contract=off` for every unix
build (since #2869) reduced the macOS differences from 8 to 3 models.

2026-10-16 (maintainer: is RG4.1 resolved now?): Linux is - `UnresolvedOnLinux()` is empty. macOS is not yet known:
the suite passes since the Eigen plugin, but the 8 entries of `UnresolvedOnMacOS()` do not count for the exit code,
and the newest Mac log in tmp/MacOS is of 1.13.75. tmp/MacOS/MacAgentRequest6.md asks for the log of a full run;
the entries that match are then removed, and RG4.1 closes when the list is empty or every remaining entry has its
cause.

#### **CLOSED_RG4.1.3** Test the math library and uninitialized values as causes, each with a build switch

CLOSED 2026-10-16 (#2954): the math library was the cause on Linux (RG4.1.3.4); no uninitialized values were needed.

- the transcendental functions (`atan2`, `sin`, `exp`, `pow`, ...) differ in the last bit between glibc, the MSVC
  UCRT and Apple's libm; `sqrt` does not. Test: route them through one header (`EXUmath::Atan2`, ...) with a switch
  to portable implementations, rerun the suite; the entries that vanish were caused by them;
- `SlimVectorBase`, `ConstSizeVectorBase`, `ConstSizeMatrixBase` and members without initializer leave values
  uninitialized. Test: a switch that fills default-constructed linalg objects with NaN; on Linux valgrind or
  `-fsanitize=memory` on the affected models;
- not checked yet: the iteration order of unordered containers and unstable sorts with equal keys.

## **OPEN_RG5** Performance

Measurement first, then the code that is actually hot.

Remark (maintainer 2026-11-09, from RG11.7, #2746): objects that compute from the coordinates passed in instead of reading them from their nodes would simplify automatic differentiation and could speed up the finite elements; done only as a measured performance step of this group, together with the numerical differentiation of the Jacobians (evaluation in the log of revision2026b step RG15.1).

Remark (2026-11-10, #2884): the 3D joints have analytic derivatives since revision2026c step RG11.6.3; what remains of their Jacobian and reaction forces is the kinematics and Jacobians of the markers and their projection.

### **CLOSED_RG5.1** Write a linear algebra micro-benchmark that every build carries

`exudyn.special.RunLinalgBenchmark()`: the real operations - `Vector` add, scale and `MultAdd`, `SlimVector` and
`Matrix3D` products, `ConstSizeMatrix` and matrix-vector products - over a size sweep across
`ResizableVectorParallelThreadingLimit`, single- and multithreaded, compiled into every build (pattern:
`src/Linalg/symbolicCppDemo.h`). `tools/benchmarks/avx2Benchmark.py` stays the solver-level counterpart. From #2397.

The results of the benchmark, and a run before and after each step that changes src/Linalg: docs/dev/design/linalgBenchmark.md.

Every run of the benchmark covers the regular and the fast module (maintainer 2026-11-11: both cases must be known); the linear algebra that matters is that of small systems - the local residuals and Jacobians of objects, markers and connectors, below 500 - and the hand-over of the local Jacobians to Eigen's sparse solvers; Eigen is the reference; local optimizations inside the linalg classes first, global changes second.

CLOSED 2026-11-12 with RG5.1.2 and RG5.1.4: the benchmark stays in every build; the evidence for the fixed-size matrices goes on in RG5.2.

### **OPEN_RG5.2** Make the hot linear algebra vectorizable with compile-time sizes

The solver time of long-vector models sits in `ODE2RHS` - per-object 3x3 and short-vector work - and
`ConstSizeMatrix` carries its size at run time, so it cannot be unrolled. Candidates: compile-time sizes, homogeneous
transformations in the rigid-body kinematics, the object loop of `ODE2RHS`; steered by RG5.1.

Maintainer 2026-11-12: an evaluation and a recommendation first; one point is certainly matrices of fixed rows and columns instead of ConstSizeMatrix, which reserves a size but can take any size during the computation (handy, but slow). Evaluation 2026-11-12: docs/dev/design/linalgFixedSize.md - after the local recommendations L1-L6 of RG5.1 the 3x3 products are as fast as a fixed-size prototype; remaining gaps of compile-time sizes: 6x6 products 2.2 times, 6x6 times vector 1.7, J^T K J at 3 x 6 1.7, short ConstSizeVector operations 3-4 times; Eigen fixed sizes rejected (3.3.7/5.0.1 with MSVC slower than the prototype). Recommended: first a profile of ODE2RHS in the rigid-body chains (bodies, markers, connectors), then SlimMatrix<T, rows, columns> with conversions to ConstSizeMatrix, Matrix6D first, Matrix3D behind a compile switch, the rigid body templated on its rotation coordinates only if the profile shows its share; proposed sub-steps in the note, waiting for the maintainer.

#161, #710 from RG5.1.4.

### **CLOSED_RG5.7** Find the slowdown of generalContactSpheresPerf.py between 1.13.190 and 1.13.309 by its timers and a bisection of the builds; a simple fix right away

#3232 (maintainer 2026-11-02: the analysis first, a simple fix right away).

Analysis 2026-11-02 (same machine, builds side by side): the slowdown is smaller than the performance logs of different days suggest - generalContactSpheresPerf with one thread 4.27 s at 1.13.190 against 4.59 s now (+7.5 %), of which the contact +0.1 s (3.17 / 3.27 s, Contact:Overall) and the 50000 loads +0.2 s. A model of 50000 mass points with a LoadForceVector each, 300 steps of explicit Euler: ODE2RHS 2.29 s at 1.13.190, 2.82 s now; without the loads unchanged. The bisection gives d0ef1944 (#2978, the symbolic user functions in the threads): 2.18 s before, 2.88 s after, about 45 ns more per evaluated load (30 % of a load). Its source changes nothing in the serial path of the loads (ComputeODE2LoadsRHS, ComputeODE2SingleLoad, CLoad::GetLoadVector and the marker functions are the same): a virtual GetUserFunctions in CLoad, CMarker, CNode, CObject, UserFunctionBase as base class of the user functions, a bool in CSystemData, the include of PythonUserFunctions.h in CSystem.cpp. So it is the generated code (inlining, layout with whole-program optimization), not an added computation; no simple fix found. Proposed fix: the loop over the loads on data precomputed at Assemble per load (its marker type, the coordinate index or the ltg, the load type, bodyFixed, the user function), so that the common load on a node needs no virtual call of the marker; measured on the model above.

CLOSED 2026-11-03 (#3232 closed, won't fix; maintainer 2026-11-03): not relevant now - the new contact module will treat the rigid bodies (particles), their loads and markers in a completely different and more efficient way, possibly with CUDA. The precomputed load data is not done.

### **CLOSED_RG5.8** Total coordinates, only if they give a performance gain

From group RG13 (closed 2026-11-09; maintainer: total coordinates only if a performance gain is expected). Total coordinates (reference plus current) beside the Current and Reference configurations, in the system data, the solvers, the items and the user functions. Today every item adds the reference to the current coordinates itself; the total coordinates would be formed once per update, at the cost of one vector addition and its memory. Only done if the baseline shows that the repeated additions cost more than that. The decision between total and relative coordinates in the user functions (RG7.3.4 point 13, userFunctionBoundary.md section 10) follows from it.

CLOSED 2026-11-12: the baseline measured (RG5.8.1), total coordinates not done (RG5.8.2).

### **CLOSED_RG5.9** Sparse solvers: MKL PARDISO loaded at run time, the threads of the factorization, the back-substitution

Maintainer 2026-11-11: MKL installed by the user (e.g. pip) and used by the C++ through the API, not in the wheel, for the performance gains; the problems of PARDISO determined in an extra study - possibly the format, or other causes; resolvable at least for some classes of problems; the solver checked at least at one of the first steps that it produces a useful factorization. Exudyn's threads cannot be passed to the solvers: for a large system the Exudyn threads idle or stopped, the factorization multithreaded, then back to the Exudyn threads - ideally the solver takes the existing threads. The back-substitution is serial and done much more often than the factorization - whether it can be done by hand at least for Eigen. Design note: [sparseSolvers.md](../dev/design/sparseSolvers.md).

Maintainer 2026-11-11, later: the sleeping workers cost something for very small thread tasks, and how long they sleep must be decided (as in NGsolve's TaskManager), so at least a setting switches them on and off - exudyn.special for such linear algebra settings during testing; with a constant step size the Jacobian hardly changes in realistic models, so the back-substitutions are many and a parallel one makes sense; RG5.9.5 closed, not done: realistic models (a large ropeway, a complex machine) have no decoupled components, and a user who has them can split them into systems of one SystemContainer - solving those in parallel is a step of its own (RG5.10), critical for the resources shared in the SystemContainer; RG5.9.6 to be measured on the performance tests and the test models with sparse solvers (expected to help little); RG5.9.2-RG5.9.4 now.

Maintainer 2026-11-11, on the next steps: no special tricks for PARDISO (such as factorizing with PARDISO and substituting with SparseLU) - the option is valuable as it is, also where it is slower; PARDISO is expected to pay for very large systems, which come with the planned 4- and 10-node tetrahedra (not a step). RG5.9.7 to be tried; the persistent threads of the TaskManager as RG5.9.8 instead of sleeping workers by default.

#### **CLOSED_RG5.9.1** Design and evaluate MKL PARDISO at run time, the threads of the factorization and a parallel back-substitution

#3427. CLOSED 2026-11-11 (#3427): docs/dev/design/sparseSolvers.md - MKL loaded at run time from the environment (no build dependency), the solver type PardisoMKL with Eigen's matrix passed without a copy, a residual check and the fallback to SparseLU, the settings; the study of the failures (half of the stored entries are numerical noise, condition up to 2e11); the back-substitution has about 3 unknowns per level in chains - not parallelizable by levels; the workers sleeping during a factorization, and independent subsystems on Exudyn's own threads. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-9-1)

#### **CLOSED_RG5.9.7** Substitute forward and backward in parallel on Exudyn's threads with a nested-dissection ordering

#3432. The ordering by nested dissection gives the triangular solves of chains 40-100 levels instead of 1000-4500, and speedup bounds of 18-60 at about twice the fill (sparseSolvers.md section 4); an ordering without METIS, the factors in a plain format after each factorization, the substitution by subtrees of the elimination tree; measured against the serial solve of SparseLU on the chains and on a model with constant Jacobian and modified Newton.

CLOSED 2026-11-11 (#3432), prototype, experimental: exu.special.solver.parallelSubstitution (default False), parallelSubstitutionPivotThreshold (1e-3), parallelSubstitutionSerial; src/Linalg/ParallelSubstitution.h/.cpp - Eigen's SparseLU with a nested-dissection ordering on the Jacobian pattern joined with the cliques of the coordinates of each object (GeneralMatrix::SetStructuralGroups, as exact zeros are dropped), unknowns with a zero diagonal lifted into the highest tree node of their neighbours with a nonzero diagonal, the factors copied into rows, the substitution by the tree depths on the threads, a factor that couples subtrees solved serially. Solves 1.8-4.4 times faster than SparseLU on 8 threads, of which 1.6-2.8 from the rows alone; factorization slower on several threads; modified Newton on chains of 1000-10000 bodies 9-13 % faster in total, small systems and full Newton slower; test model parallelSubstitutionTest.py. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-9-7)

#### **CLOSED_RG5.9.8** Keep persistent worker threads in the TaskManager, started by exudyn.special.solver and sleeping between solves

#3437. Maintainer 2026-11-11: sleeping workers by default would change the workflow of the TaskManager; instead a function of exudyn.special.solver starts a number of workers that stay - for many runs of small systems; the solvers still take the number of threads from simulationSettings, and where the two disagree simulationSettings wins and a message is written. Between solves the workers sleep on the condition variable of RG5.9.3; the raytracer takes them like a solver.

CLOSED 2026-11-11 (#3437): exu.special.solver.StartPersistentThreads(n), StopPersistentThreads(), PersistentThreads(); a parked task manager (detached from task_manager, GetNumThreads() 1 outside a computation, workers asleep on the condition variable of RG5.9.3); TaskManager::AcquireWorkers / ReleaseWorkers with a ticket for the solvers, the raytracer and the linear algebra benchmark - persistent workers if the number agrees, otherwise threads of its own with a warning, and the persistent ones again after it; an atexit hook stops them; 0.2-0.8 ms saved per solve (40-70 % of a solve of one step, 4-8 % of 20 steps of 2000 bodies); test model persistentThreadsTest.py. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-9-8)

#### **CLOSED_RG5.9.9** Solve with the rows of the factors of SparseLU instead of Eigen's supernodal solve

#3441. Proposed from RG5.9.7: the serial substitution by the rows of the factors was 1.6-2.8 times faster than the supernodal solve of Eigen on chains; for every EigenSparse factorization (COLAMD), for modified Newton with many solves per factorization; the copy of the factors costs about 0.3 ms per factorization of 13000 unknowns; measured on the performance and test models with sparse solvers before it becomes the default.

CLOSED 2026-11-11 (#3441): SparseLURows<Ordering> (Eigen's SparseLU with the factors copied into rows after each factorization, factorize invalidates them) is the solver of EigenSparse and the base of the parallel substitution; exu.special.solver.sparseSolveByRows (default True) switches back to Eigen's solve. Performance models 22.21 -> 21.68 s (perf3DRigidBodies -18 %), 52 test models 21.23 -> 20.64 s (up to -11 %), no model clearly slower. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-9-9)

#### **CLOSED_RG5.9.10** Write down where a new setting goes: simulationSettings and visualizationSettings, exudyn.special for experimental flags

#3446. Maintainer 2026-11-12, on the flags of exudyn.special added in RG5.9: these are experimental flags or flags that do not belong to the simulationSettings (operations outside the solver routines); otherwise settings are preferably in simulationSettings or visualizationSettings, which keeps them consistent and reproducible. Not a hard rule, as there are exceptions, but the common way, in CLAUDE.md and CODING_STYLE.md.

CLOSED 2026-11-12 (#3446): CODING_STYLE.md section 1 and CLAUDE.md (a new section 'The common way - not hard rules'): a new setting goes into simulationSettings or visualizationSettings; exudyn.special for experimental flags and for what acts outside the solver routines or across simulations; a setting put into exudyn.special for another reason says why in its description.

#### **CLOSED_RG5.9.11** linearSolver.dropThreshold: leave out the near-zero entries of the sparse Newton Jacobian

#3448. Maintainer 2026-11-12: the near-zero entries of the sparse matrices get a threshold. In the chains of rigid bodies and the contact model of solverBaseline.py, 14-55 % of the nonzeros of the Newton Jacobian are below 1e-12 times the largest entry of their row and their column.

CLOSED 2026-11-12 (#3448): simulationSettings.linearSolver.dropThreshold (default 0: only exact zeros), relative to the smaller of the largest magnitudes of the row and the column, applied by GeneralMatrixEigenSparse::FinalizeMatrix (Eigen prune) to the Newton Jacobian only. Measured with 1e-12 (scratch dropBench.py): rigid body spring-damper chain of 50 bodies with full Newton 13-17 % faster (factorization 0.266 -> 0.150 s), revolute chain of 50 bodies with full Newton 13 % slower (factorization 0.136 -> 0.163 s: the ordering of the pattern without the small entries is worse), modified Newton unchanged; Newton iterations and results equal to 1e-13 everywhere. So the default stays 0. Test model linearSolverDropThresholdTest.py. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-9-11)

### **OPEN_RG5.10** Solve the systems of one SystemContainer in parallel, each on its own thread

#3433. Approved by the maintainer 2026-11-11 (in place of RG5.9.5): a model of independent parts is split by the user into systems of one SystemContainer; first a study of what the systems share (renderer and visualization data, Python user functions and the GIL, output files, the TaskManager, pySpecial and other globals), then a design.

Maintainer 2026-11-11: the pre-analysis of what the systems share now.

Maintainer 2026-11-11, on the concept given in the chat: RG5.10 needs a design file and is bigger than thought, still good to continue; more general than a SolveDynamicParallel - the main gain is a parallel solve of loosely coupled systems (several machines or robots in a factory handling common objects); a common mapping of the results for the solution file and the SolutionViewer; two starting points to compare, several systems of one SystemContainer and decoupled parts of one system solved in parallel; for several systems, a super-system that receives the coordinates of the subsystems after each synchronization step and is the one drawn and written; a solver of its own for each subsystem, so that implicit-explicit coupling works at once. The revised concept: docs/dev/design/coupledSystems.md, to be evaluated by the maintainer.

HALTED 2026-11-12 (maintainer): unclear whether it is continued. Python user functions, PreStepUserFunctions and the like are only one problem of the approach; fully decoupled systems can already be solved with Python's multiprocessing library, independent Python processes without any synchronization problem - with only the results to be joined at the end, while a finer synchronization would be comparatively hard. The design note coupledSystems.md stays as the record of the concept.

#### **CLOSED_RG5.10.1** Pre-analysis: what the systems of one SystemContainer and their solvers share

#3440. CLOSED 2026-11-11 (#3440): docs/dev/design/systemContainerParallel.md - the solve never releases the GIL, so solves on Python threads run one after another today; to be made per solver or locked: the one task manager with its global thread count, thread id 0 as the main thread (symbolic recordings), the suspension of workers, the pySpecial counters, the global timers, a static matrix in MatrixBase::Invert, pout and outputBuffer, PyError/PyWarning and the global error flag, the idle operations (renderer queues, PyCheckSignals), colliding default file names, the shared settings object in SolveDynamic; safe: the per-system render lock, per-solver data, thread_local scratch data, PARDISO (serialized). The natural start: numberOfThreads = 1 per system, the systems on threads of a pool, thread-local thread counts and ids. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-9-8)

### **CLOSED_RG5.11** Raytracer: threads parallel to the solver, and its scaling with the number of threads

Approved by the maintainer 2026-11-11: the raytracer shall run in parallel to the solver from the renderer thread, and its scaling shall be understood - it needs 64-128 threads on 16 cores for its best speed, although each thread gets small portions of work; what is learned may speed up the parallel loops of Exudyn in general, and a faster raytracer helps every user.

CLOSED 2026-11-12: the raytracer runs on threads of its own (RG5.11.1), its scaling is explained (RG5.11.2), and the default takes the logical processors (RG5.11.3).

#### **CLOSED_RG5.11.1** Run the raytracer with threads of its own while a solver runs, also from the renderer thread

#3438. The raytracer is started from the main thread and from the renderer thread; the TaskManager is one global object, so during a solve the raytracer falls back to one thread, and one started in the renderer thread during a solve crashes at once. A pool of threads for the raytracer next to the solver's.

CLOSED 2026-11-12 (#3438): RaytracerParallelFor - std::threads of the raytracer's own for each image, the calling thread as thread 0, the tiles from an atomic counter, the scratch arrays by a thread_local index; no TaskManager, so the raytracer renders in parallel while a solver runs, from a user function and from the renderer thread; the persistent threads of #3437 are no longer used by it; the same speed (1200 x 1000: 0.356 s on 32 threads against 0.371 s); test model raytracerDuringSolveTest.py. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-11-1)

#### **CLOSED_RG5.11.2** Measure and explain the scaling of the raytracer with the number of threads

#3439. Best speed only with 64-128 threads on 16 cores. Candidates: the static split of the tiles when load balancing is off (multiThreadingLoadBalancing is taken from the last solver), the tiles per thread, the workers that spin with yield, the memory traffic of the tiles; the result applies to ParallelFor in general.

CLOSED 2026-11-11 (#3439): measured (raytracerNOGLFWtest.py scene, 1200 x 1000, 16 cores / 32 logical processors): with load balancing 4.66 s on 1 thread, 0.664 s on 8, 0.437 s on 16, 0.372 s on 32, no gain beyond (12.6 times); without load balancing each thread renders a fixed band of tiles - 0.585 s on 16, 0.471 on 32, best 0.421 s on 64 threads, the oversubscription balancing a little: the observed need for 64-128 threads. The raytracer took the flag from exudyn.special.solver.multiThreadingLoadBalancing, which every solver overwrites with its parallel.useLoadBalancing; it now always balances its tiles (AcquireWorkers with a load balancing mode): 0.369 s on 32 threads either way. For Exudyn in general: a ParallelFor without load balancing splits statically and suffers from unequal work. The default raytracer.numberOfThreads = 8 leaves half the speed on a machine like this (0.664 against 0.37 s). [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-9-9)

#### **CLOSED_RG5.11.3** raytracer.numberOfThreads = 0, the number of logical processors, as the default

#3445. Maintainer 2026-11-12, after RG5.11.2: the default 8 gave 0.664 s against 0.37 s with the 32 logical processors of the measuring machine; 0 shall select the number of logical processors automatically and be the default.

CLOSED 2026-11-12 (#3445): visualizationSettings.raytracer.numberOfThreads: minimum 0, default 0 = std::thread::hardware_concurrency(), clamped to 1-256 as before (Raytracing.cpp); parameterConversionTestReference moved numberOfThreads to the settings with minimum 0; revisions.md.

### **CLOSED_RG5.12** GeneralContact: the arrays allocated in every step

#3454. The allocation count of solverBaseline.py (RG5.8.1) shows about 100 array allocations per step in the GeneralContact model, while the other models allocate only during the initialization. Maintainer 2026-11-12: find the cause; if it is straightforward, solve it at once.

CLOSED 2026-11-12 (#3454), no change: the arrays are the cells of the search tree, each allocated when a sphere first enters it and grown when it first holds more; only in the first solve (100 per step), a second solve from the initial state 0.15-0.7 per step, both in the same time. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-12)

### **CLOSED_RG6.22** Buttons and sliders in the render window, with one Python callback

Maintainer 2026-11-09: buttons and sliders with labels directly in the render window, like the icon bar, instead of a tkinter dialog. This would also simplify the interactive robot model of RG19.16. Each has transparency, a centered text, a font size and a location `[[x0, y0], [x1, y1]]` relative to the window, so that it scales when the window is resized. All of them call one Python callback, like the key press function, with the ID of the element and its state as a Vector3D (`state = data[0]`), so that the interface can grow.

**Recommendation (to be decided before the implementation):**
- **An API of the renderer, not visualization settings.** The settings hold settings, and the elements change at run time. The calls would be `SC.renderer.AddButton(...)` and `AddSlider(...)`, each returning its ID; `SetControlState(id, data)` and `GetControlState(id)`; `DeleteControl(id)` and `ClearControls()`; and `SetControlUserFunction(f)` with `f(id, data)`. Each element is also its dict, with `type`, `location`, `text`, `fontSize`, `color`, `backgroundColor` (alpha = transparency), and for a slider `minMax`, `value` and `format` (the value in the label, e.g. `'q1 = {:.2f}'`); a button is either `push` or `toggle`. The elements go into `SC.renderer.GetDictionary`/`SetDictionary`.
- **Location** as for the scopes: `[[x0, y0], [x1, y1]]` relative to the window from the top left corner (`StdArray22F`); the font size scales with the display scaling.
- **The mouse**: the icon bar first, then the elements, then the scopes and the view, so that a click on an element never rotates the model. A slider is dragged with the left button or changed by the mouse wheel, and a button acts on release.
- **The callback** follows the path of the key press function (the renderer thread queues the event, the main thread calls the function). `data = [state, 0, 0]`: 0/1 for a button (a push button sends 1 on release), the value for a slider; the two other components are reserved.
- **In images:** they are drawn after the frame is saved, as the icon bar, so they are not in saved or raytraced images; `general.iconBarAndControlsInImages` (default False, maintainer 2026-11-10) puts them and the icon bar into the images; the scopes have their own switch.
- **Python helper:** a small class in `exudyn.interactive` that maps IDs to Python functions, so that a model writes `controls.Slider('q1', ..., function=...)`; the robot model of RG19.16 is its first user.

Sub-steps when decided: (1) the element list, drawing and mouse in C++, (2) the Python API, callback and dictionary, with a test of the API without a window, (3) the helper class and RG19.16 on it, (4) the documentation in the GUI chapter.

CLOSED 2026-11-10 (#3400 resolved; maintainer 2026-11-10: decided with the corrections above): RendererControls.h, the drawing and the mouse in GlfwClientExtended.cpp, the queue in rendererPythonInterface.cpp, SC.renderer.AddButton ... SetControlUserFunction, GetDictionary 'controls', general.iconBarAndControlsInImages, exudyn.interactive.WindowControls, rendererControlsTest.py, renderWindowControls.py, GUI.md; RG19.16 stays its own step. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg6-22)

### **CLOSED_RG6.23** Zoom and drag of the scopes while the simulation is idle

Maintainer 2026-11-09: while the simulation is idle (paused or stopped), the mouse zooms and drags the lines of a scope it is over. A double click, or the simulation continuing, resets the view to the stored one. The switching probably belongs to the render state, like the other mouse interactions.

**Recommendation (to be decided before the implementation):**
- **Idle** means: no solver is running, or the solver is paused (SPACE), or the solution viewer's animation is paused. The renderer reads this from the flags it already has for the pause and the running simulation; there is no new setting.
- **Interaction** while the mouse is over a scope region: the wheel zooms about the cursor, with SHIFT only x and with CTRL only y; dragging with the left button moves the lines; a double click resets. Outside the scopes, or while running, the mouse does what it does now.
- **Where the view is kept**: in the render state of the view, per scope, as an override of the ranges (`[xmin, xmax, ymin, ymax]`, empty = none). It is not in `VSettingsScope.minMaxX/minMaxY`, so that the stored settings stay what the user wrote. `ScopePlot::AddScope` takes the override in place of its ranges (section 2 of docs/dev/internals/scope.md); OpenGL and the raytracer draw the same.
- **Reset**: when the simulation runs again (the next time step), with a double click, and when the settings of the scope change.
- The tick labels follow the zoomed range, as they follow the ranges now.

Sub-steps when decided: (1) the idle flag and the override in the render state, ScopePlot with the override, (2) the mouse handling with its priority after the icon bar and the controls of the step on buttons and sliders, (3) the user documentation (GUI chapter, scopes) and an interactive example.

CLOSED 2026-11-10 (#3401 resolved): idle = paused or no new state for 0.3 s, a new state resets; RenderState::scopeViews with the override and the last drawn ranges and plot areas; wheel, SHIFT, CTRL, drag and double click; GUI.md and the mouse table. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg6-22)

### **CLOSED_RG6.25** Hide the graphics of chosen systems in a view: view[0-3].scene.hideMbs

#3442. Requested by the maintainer 2026-11-11: different things in different views; first the evaluation whether this is problematic or inefficient for the renderer.

Evaluation (2026-11-11, from the code): no problem and no cost. Each system has its own GraphicsData (its VisualizationSystem), and the renderer's graphicsDataList holds one pointer per system, appended in the order of SC.AddSystem (VisualizationSystemContainer::Append), so its index is the number of the mbs. Nothing is collected per view: all views draw the same list in GlfwRenderer::RenderGraphicsData(viewID), one loop over the systems. Hiding is a test per system and view in that loop - no filtering when the graphics are collected, no copy, and a hidden system even saves drawing time. Inside the same loop are the faces, edges, lines, points, texts and spheres, the shadow volumes (DrawTrianglesWithShadow per GraphicsData) and the picking with the mouse (selection mode), so they follow at once. Outside it, and to be filtered alike for a consistent view: zoom all and zoom to an item (RenderState::ComputeMaxSceneSize and ComputeZoomToItem over the list), the sensor traces (Raytracer::SensorTraceLines over all systems; a trace belongs to the system of its sensor) and the raytracer of the view (Raytracer::SoftwareRenderer(viewID), which builds its scene from GetGraphicsDataList()). Shared by all systems and not affected: the scopes, the contour color bar, the lights, the world basis.

Recommendation: a member hideMbs of VSettingsScene - so view0.scene.hideMbs ... view3.scene.hideMbs - of type TArrayIndex(size=-1) with the default [] (the type is used in the visualization settings already); a list of system numbers rather than of MainSystems, as the settings are plain data; numbers beyond the systems are ignored. One helper IsHidden(viewID, systemIndex) used in the five places above. A test model with two systems and hideMbs in view 1, checked by the raytracer images of view 0 and view 1 (no window) or by the graphics data the raytracer receives.

CLOSED 2026-11-11 (#3442): view[0-3].scene.hideMbs (TArrayIndex, default []); SystemHiddenInView(viewID, system, settings) in GlfwRenderer::RenderGraphicsData (faces, edges, lines, points, texts, shadows, picking), RenderState::ComputeMaxSceneSize/ComputeZoomToItem (GraphicsData::ComputeMaxScene with the hidden systems), Raytracer::SensorTraceLines (viewID) and Raytracer::SoftwareRenderer (triangles, lines, texts); test model viewHideMbsTest.py with raytracer images against containers holding only the shown systems. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg5-9-9)

### **OPEN_RG6.26** Raytracer: the cost of shadows and soft shadows, and the options to reduce it

#3460. Maintainer 2026-11-12: the raytracer got faster, but lightRadius variations are handled very inefficiently, possibly other things as well; better options, in particular for shadows - analyze first, the analysis and the suggestions in a design file raytracerImprovements.md. The proposed sub-steps are in its section 5, for the maintainer to choose.

Maintainer 2026-11-12: the proposed speedup is very good, into the plan (RG6.26.2-RG6.26.12); the three correctness points as issues and steps (RG6.26.13-RG6.26.15); blurred reflections if doable during the improvements (RG6.26.16); start with the first steps.

#### **CLOSED_RG6.26.1** Analysis: how shadows are computed, measurements, inefficiencies, options

#3460. CLOSED 2026-11-12: docs/dev/design/raytracerImprovements.md - per-pixel soft shadow 0.0206 s per sample (1200 x 1000, 32 threads; 13 samples 0.355 s against 0.106 s for a hard shadow), the low-resolution precomputation 1/9 of that but with about 30 ms serial smoothing and upsampling; the precomputation also runs without a light radius (+24 %); proposed order: bitwise improvements (parallel copy-free filtering, separate hit test and shading, mailboxing, ray length), precomputation only with a light radius, adaptive penumbra sampling, stratified samples, occluder cache, flat grid; three correctness points (absolute shadow-ray offset, transparent objects cast full shadows, no soft shadows for directional lights).

[log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg6-26-1)

## **OPEN_RG8** Compiled C++ user items

Plugins: user items compiled against the shipped headers. A plugin author builds Exudyn from source once and the
plugin with the same toolchain, so compiler, runtime and flags are identical and no C-ABI shim is needed. From
revision2026 phase R9.

### **OPEN_RG8.1** Move the item registry into one exported translation unit so that plugins share it

`MainObjectFactory.h` keeps its singleton in a function-local static of a header-only template, so every binary
has its own copy; the dispatch needs no change. #1140, #1241.

### **OPEN_RG8.2** Check an ABI fingerprint when a plugin registers, and refuse a mismatch with a message

Exudyn version, macro set, compiler and version, `__cplusplus`, on MSVC `_ITERATOR_DEBUG_LEVEL` and the runtime
model; `sizeof` canaries of `Vector`, `CObject`, `std::string`, `std::function`. The Debug/Release runtime case
matters most.

### **OPEN_RG8.3** Build a reference plugin with every build and test it in a system

A trivial registered object, a handshake assertion and a test that solves with it - so interface drift breaks the
build, not a user's plugin.

### **OPEN_RG8.4** Ship the plugin headers in the wheel and add exudyn.get_include()

Headers only: `Linalg/`, `Utilities/`, the item base classes, the plugin interface.

### **OPEN_RG8.5** Report duplicate item names at registration instead of aborting the import

`RegisterClass` throws today, so two plugins with the same name break `import exudyn`.

### **OPEN_RG8.6** Discover plugins at import from a directory, a variable and entry points, each loaded in isolation

`~/.exudyn/plugins/`, `EXUDYN_PLUGIN_PATH`, `importlib.metadata` entry points; a manifest read before loading; a
broken plugin never breaks `import exudyn`.

### **OPEN_RG8.7** Record the loaded plugins and their versions in the solver log and the solution file header

### **OPEN_RG8.8** Generate a plugin skeleton from a user's definition file

C++ skeleton, Python dict-building class and `.pyi` stub, emitted into the user's package.

### **OPEN_RG8.9** Document what a plugin may and must do

Never unloaded or reloaded; built from source; its item class imported explicitly (`from myplugin import ...`); the
member functions a C++ item must provide (#1139, checked when it compiles by RG9.6).

### **OPEN_RG8.10** Let an item take a user function compiled in C++

#1767 (prototypes and registration), #1768 (a pybind object that holds them), #1769 (the capabilities of
`PythonUserFunctionBase`), #1751 (the injection through the item); after RG8.1 to RG8.3.

## **OPEN_RG9** Structural core improvements

The architecture of the core, where one change touches every item; the HUGE issues are its backlog (#354 automatic
differentiation, #1104 the generic object, #1722 `exudyn.Parameter`, #88 data dependencies, #1821 kinematics and #1822
inverse-dynamics solvers). This is where **2.0** comes from (info document D15). Put off: the energy of contact and
special objects (#2202).

### **CLOSED_RG9.6** Check at compile time that an item provides the member functions its declarations require

#2866. Abstract base classes do not fit - what an item must provide depends on what it declares -, so the generated
header of each item gets a `static_assert` per required function with `decltype(&CItem::F)`, which names the class
that declares `F`. The evaluation is in the log of revision2026b step RG9.6.

CLOSED 2026-11-11 (#2866): the item headers check the required member functions when they compile. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg9-6-2)

classes.

#### **CLOSED_RG9.11.18** Evaluate the derivatives of M(q) q_tt and of C_q^T lambda by q as options of the Newton Jacobian

From RG9.11.7: neither is in the Newton Jacobians, also not in the system-wide numerical one, which differentiates the forces. d(M(q) q_tt)/dq is zero for the bodies with a constant mass matrix, e.g. ObjectRigidBody on Lie group nodes, and may matter for ObjectKinematicTree and larger objects (maintainer 2026-11-02); its effect is measured by #3228 (low priority, no step), with the exact Jacobian of SymbolicSystem. d(C_q^T lambda)/dq can matter more; jacobianConnectorDerivative covers d(J^T f)/dq of the connectors, not the constraints. Proposed: an option of newton.numericalDifferentiation, False by default, that adds d(C_q^T lambda)/dq by a local numerical differentiation of the reaction forces of each constraint by its own coordinates, serially; later, for the joints whose reaction forces are the marker Jacobian times a constant direction, by ComputeMarkerDataJacobianDerivative, as for the connectors, in parallel.

CLOSED 2026-11-02 (#3230 resolved, #3201 stays open): constraintReactionDerivative (False), numerical per item, serially; fourBarMechanismIftomm 1058 / 802 iterations with full Newton, the other constrained models unchanged at 1.4 to 2.6 times the time; d(M q_tt)/dq by #3228. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg9-11-18)

## **OPEN_RG12** Symbolic

`exudyn.symbolic` and its user functions (maintainer 2026-10-12): the interface of the symbolic user functions was the
first design, before some symbolic methods existed; it is revised, with the dense linear algebra fully in symbolic and
access to the MainSystem, before symbolic user items (RG7.8) are built on it.

### **CLOSED_RG12.1** Describe the status of exudyn.symbolic and its user functions

CLOSED 2026-10-16 (#2981): [internals/symbolic.md](../dev/internals/symbolic.md) - recording, the expression
types, the 30 signatures, what is missing (matrix results, MainSystem access, matrix functions, parallel evaluation),
the tests. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-5)

### **CLOSED_RG12.2** Measure the user function interfaces as they are

#2982. Per signature: arguments, return types, the transfer to C++, against the Python user functions.

Measured 2026-10-18 (closed with RG12.2.2, 2026-10-25): 45 user functions in 24 signatures, 39 with a symbolic version (tools/benchmarks/userFunctionInterfaces.py);
one representative per class measured against 1.13.6 (perfUserFunctions.py with rigid6, ode1x6, mass6, preStep): no
Exudyn allocation per call, symbolic 5 to 44 times faster than Python; userFunctionBoundary.md section 13.
[log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-2)

#### **CLOSED_RG12.2.1** A symbolic user function that returns a constant

CLOSED 2026-10-18 (#3038): `return True` of a preStepUserFunction failed with a cast error when recorded; a constant
result is a symbolic constant. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-2)

#### **CLOSED_RG12.2.2** Copy the fixed-size vector and matrix arguments of symbolic user functions in one pass

CLOSED 2026-10-25 (#3039; maintainer 2026-10-25: measure; worth it if the code does not grow much and the speedup is
about 10 %): measured first - skipping the copy of the 90 matrix values gives 0.58 -> 0.455 us per call; copying them
in one pass instead of by components gives 0.43 with the vectors, without new state; copying only on change or
reading in place would gain about 6 % more, not done. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-2-2)

#### **CLOSED_RG12.2.3** Find the memory allocations in every step of a symbolic ObjectUserBody

CLOSED 2026-10-19 (#3046; maintainer: userRigidBodyTest.py with 10000 steps reports 613350 allocations): three
causes, all removed - `MatrixBase::CopyFrom` reallocated also at the same size, the result cache of ObjectUserBody
stored a Matrix through a temporary ResizableMatrix, and ObjectUserBody had local matrices and vectors; 0 allocations
per step now, for Python and symbolic user functions. Not the cause of the time: 1.86 s against 0.12 s of
ObjectRigidBody, see RG12.2.4. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-2-3)

#### **CLOSED_RG12.2.4** Evaluate a shared node of a symbolic expression once per evaluation

#3047: a recorded expression is a graph, and its shared nodes - the sin and cos of the angles, the components of
omegaLocal - are evaluated again at every use; the force of userRigidBodyTest.py is a text of 9456 characters and
takes about 5 microseconds. Proposal: a node used more than once keeps its value with the number of the evaluation,
a counter raised when the arguments are set; then measure userRigidBodyTest.py with 10000 steps again.

CLOSED 2026-10-19 (#3047, won't fix; maintainer: against the proposal and against any hashing of values - a design
decision, info document D18): values are recomputed; the symbolic rigid body is a demo and test case and stays as it is.
[log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-2-4)

#### **CLOSED_RG12.2.5** Evaluate the options for intermediate values in symbolic expressions

#3048 (maintainer 2026-10-20): an automatic value per tree node, statements and loops, variables by hand, others; the
scope of variables; whether the C++ items can be recorded. CLOSED 2026-10-20:
[internals/symbolic.md](../dev/internals/symbolic.md) recommends a flat evaluation of the
recorded graph (the force of userRigidBodyTest.py: 122 nodes, 1171 operations as a tree), and finds the connectors,
joints and ANCF elements recordable through their AD templates; waiting for the decision.
[log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-2-5)

#### **CLOSED_RG12.2.6** Flat evaluation of the scalar expressions, with the list as text

CLOSED 2026-10-20 (#3053; maintainer: implement the flat evaluation for scalar expressions, then vectors and matrices,
with the tests; allow export of the compiled lists as text; no C++ recording now): `FlatProgram`, the operand
interface of the 39 scalar expression classes, `flatEvaluation` and `GetFlatProgram()`; test_symbolicFlatEvaluation.py.
[log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-2-6)

#### **CLOSED_RG12.2.7** Flat evaluation of the vector and matrix operations

CLOSED 2026-10-20 (#3054): the vector and matrix nodes are operations of the list (`FlatNode`, `FlatValue`), the
interface generated for 21 vector and matrix classes; components of arguments written inline in the text;
userRigidBodyTest.py with 10000 steps, symbolic body alone: 2.72 s (tree) -> 0.75 s (flat).
[log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-2-7)

### **CLOSED_RG12.3** Design improved user function interfaces, with the dense linear algebra in symbolic

CLOSED 2026-10-17 (#2983, #2996): vectors and matrices are numpy views for Python user functions and named vectors
and matrices of symbolic ones, results of any size without allocation, matrix results dense or sparse; the steps of
userFunctionBoundary.md section 11 are done.

#### **CLOSED_RG12.3.1** Pre-analyze the allocations of vectors at the C++/Python boundary of the user functions

CLOSED 2026-10-15 (#2996 stays open for the options; maintainer: Python user functions run serially, no GIL
question): [internals/userFunctions.md](../dev/internals/userFunctions.md). 14 of 26 user function signatures
take `StdVector`; about a dozen heap allocations per call, most of them copies by value; a symbolic call with vectors
0.31 microseconds against 0.04 with scalars, Python lists (not numpy) 0.25 plus 25 ns per value. Options A to E,
proposal: #2998, then A, then C with RG7.8, D as a decision of its own. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-3-1)

#### **CLOSED_RG12.3.2** Keep the heap memory of a ResizableConstVector above 7 items when its size is set again

CLOSED 2026-10-15 (#2998): the data stays in its memory while it fits, every allocation records the heap size; unit
tests under AddressSanitizer, test model symbolicVectorUserFunctionTest.py (10 coordinates, symbolic = Python).
[log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-3-2)

#### **CLOSED_RG12.3.3** Propose the vector and matrix classes and the call of the user functions for Python and symbolic

CLOSED 2026-10-16 (#2996 stays open for the steps; maintainer: no allocation on the Exudyn side in both, the symbolic

#### **CLOSED_RG12.3.4** Pass the vectors of the user functions by const reference and copy them once

CLOSED 2026-10-16 (#3005; maintainer: every step measured against 1.13.6, Python and symbolic, the performance shall
not drop): const StdVector& arguments, const Args& in the symbolic Evaluate, CopyFrom of the result, the move assignment
of Vector; perfUserFunctions.py in the performance suite, userFunctionPerformance.py for the comparison; symbolic vector
functions 35-45 % faster, the rest unchanged. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-3-4-done)

#2996 (userFunctionBoundary.md step 1): const references in the signatures and constructors, `CopyFrom(std::vector)`,

#### **CLOSED_RG12.3.5** Generate an Evaluate per signature and evaluate symbolic functions into node buffers

CLOSED 2026-10-16 (#3006): every vector node evaluates into its own memory (EvaluateRef), the arguments are copied

#2996 (step 2): `Evaluate(Vector& result, ..., const Vector& q, ...)` dispatching on the kind, symbolic named variables
that link, nodes that evaluate into their own buffers.

#### **CLOSED_RG12.3.9** Give the Python user functions their fixed-size vectors and matrices as numpy arrays too

CLOSED 2026-10-16 (#3010; maintainer: numpy for the 3D and 6D vectors too, if there are no major boundaries): there is
one - built and measured, a CartesianSpringDamper user function costs twice as much with numpy arrays (1.40 against
0.66 us written with components, 1.72 written with numpy operations), as Python computes slowly with numpy scalars and
tiny arrays; so the fixed sizes stay lists, for decision. Kept: the numpy views made with the numpy C interface
(0.58 instead of 1.0 us for five views; the run-time sized vectors gain 10 %), a fixed-size result from numpy in one
pass, two examples that wrote into their argument return a new vector. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-5)

solved in vectorized form; symbolic with dot or @): the 3D/6D vectors and 3x3/6x6 matrices of Python user functions
are read-only numpy views; the scripts that take the vector form use it; `esym.Vector`/`Matrix` have `dot()` and `@`
with numpy's meaning, and numpy defers to them. The same vectorized Cartesian spring-damper function is 0.78 and the
load 0.88 of 1.13.6. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-3-10)

CLOSED 2026-11-01 (all sub-steps closed; the export of RG12.51.5 moved to RG14.5).

Maintainer 2026-10-25: from a model built of user items with symbolic user functions, the equations of motion as one
symbolic program - mass matrix, forces, constraints - for control, linearization and code generation; first ideas in
the first version of docs/dev/design/symbolicExport.md, section 7, in git at commit 9790d8c5. After the symbolic steps of RG12
are completed.

#### **CLOSED_RG12.51.1** Design the symbolic system model, in its own design file

CLOSED 2026-10-26 (#3109; maintainer 2026-10-26: a design file; the assembly by the LTG maps, sparse matrices, export
and GPU evaluation with Numba first, explicit integration by residuals per object, a generalized-alpha solver in Python
with the settings of SimulationSettings checked against the internal solvers, the Jacobians by esym.Diff):
docs/dev/design/symbolicSystem.md - per object a flat program, kinds of objects
sharing one recording, the global matrices as triplets; explicit integration with a constant mass matrix or a
matrix-free product from the residuals; the Jacobians as programs by symbolic differentiation (RG12.54); five
sub-steps and four decisions proposed. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-51-1)

Decisions (maintainer 2026-10-26, design §8): residual and (sparse) mass matrix both available, the implicit
integrators need only the residual; exudyn.symbolic and numpy first, Numba for the GPU; exudyn/misc/symbolicSystem.py
for model and solver, special, not for the broad use; symbolic differentiation into a new program as the target of
RG12.54, a Jacobian column by column by esym.Diff before it. RG12.55, the user items with the interfaces of the
built-in items, comes before the system model goes deeper (RG12.51.4).

Proposal (2026-10-31; maintainer 2026-10-31: make a proposal first) in docs/dev/design/symbolicSystem.md section 12.7: declared by esym.SparseMatrix(rows, columns, triplets) or Matrix.Sparse(), not detected; a slot kind with compressed rows, codes for A v, A^T v, s A, A + B, A[i,j], to dense; the derivative nnz x n on the pattern; equal to the dense product to the bit; triplets in the export; after RG12.65.8. Waits for the decision.

Maintainer 2026-11-01 on #3199: agreed, first in Python (RG12.65.7.1); the slot kind is RG12.65.7.2.

CLOSED 2026-11-04 as a step: .1 done, .2 with #3168, which stays open.

#### **CLOSED_RG12.65.8** The vector and matrix operations as codes of the table, evaluated in one array of the program instead of by the nodes

CLOSED 2026-10-31 (#3176): the 23 classes of vector and matrix operations are rows of the table (kind of the result, name, kinds of the operands), computed by ArrayOperation with the arithmetic of their nodes, for SymbolicProgram and for the constants of Optimize; the slots are SlotValue (number, or pointer, rows, columns), the vectors and matrices in one array of the program, laid out at the first evaluation and when a leaf changes its size; FindDerivativeRule gone, the rules are the column of the table; RecordNode the only caller of EvaluateFromOperands. The same bits as the tree; 2.40 instead of 2.61 us for a recorded 5x5 mass matrix. Design docs/dev/design/symbolicSystem.md section 12.7. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg12-65-8)

Whether some C++ item functions - the force of a connector, the constraint of a joint - can be recorded from the C++ code, with adaptations and templates on the scalar type as the automatic differentiation of the connector interface has them, so that the user item is not written again in Python.

The other way round: C++ generated from the recordings of the user items, with the vectors and matrices kept at the C++ level so that the code stays readable, and decorators in the Python code that mark the equations to be recorded.

#### **OPEN_RG12.71.3** Evaluate exporting the equations of the reference manual from the implementation of the user items

Some user items contain exactly the equations of their documentation: whether the description of an item can be exported from its user item, so that documentation and implementation stay in sync; the C++ and symbolic duplicates remain and are checked numerically.

## **CLOSED_RG13** Total coordinates

Total coordinates - reference plus current - beside the Current and Reference configurations, in the system data, the
solvers, the computation functions of the items and the user functions (maintainer 2026-10-16). The solvers keep
working on the coordinates relative to the reference; the total coordinates are an additional result, at the cost of
one vector addition per update and their memory in the cache - mostly visible in the explicit solvers. The decision
between total and relative coordinates in the user functions (RG7.3.4 point 13, userFunctionBoundary.md section 10)
waits for this group.

CLOSED 2026-11-09 (maintainer: total coordinates only if they give a performance gain): moved to RG5.8.

#3002. Rigid multibody, flexible bodies, contact, large systems; implicit and explicit solvers; dense and sparse,
modified Newton, step sizes, multithreading - so that the cost of an additional vector addition per step is visible.

CLOSED 2026-11-09: moved to RG5.8.1 (#3002).

### **CLOSED_RG13.2** Evaluate how the total coordinates are provided efficiently

#1910. After RG13.1: where they are formed (once per step, per Newton iteration, per evaluation of the right-hand
side), stored or linked (`currentState.ODE2CoordsTotal`, an option of the solver), how items and user functions get
them, what it costs in each solver; a proposal for decision.

CLOSED 2026-11-09: moved to RG5.8.2 (#1910).

## **OPEN_RG14** Uniform evaluation of symbolic systems on the CPU and the GPU

The composed recordings of a symbolic system evaluated as a fixed sequence of uniform array operations - numpy on the
CPU, CuPy or kernels on the GPU -, for one state and for ensembles of states (maintainer 2026-10-28). Design
docs/dev/design/uniformEvaluation.md. On the GPU the results are not those of Exudyn to the bit: sin, cos, sqrt of
CUDA are not the C library's, fused multiply-adds round once, sums by atomic adds differ between runs; the comparison
is by a tolerance.

docs/dev/design/uniformEvaluation.md. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg14-1)

### **OPEN_RG19.18** ProfileJointPath: no line of length zero between two blends

#3411 (maintainer 2026-11-11: an issue and a step; the warning of robotPathPlanningTest.py comes from the planning part, which the visualization did not change). The cause is in _Segments: the exact comparison np.any(end != start) keeps a line of length about 1e-17 where two blends meet at the middle of a segment, as on the paths of RRT-Connect with equally spaced waypoints; then h = 0 in Largest and np.diff(p) = 0 in pDDot. Fix by a tolerance relative to the path length; a test that no interval of p has length zero.

## **OPEN_RG20** Advanced solvers

Maintainer 2026-11-04: an index-3 trapezoidal solver with GGL stabilization and an adaptive step, a 4th-order Lobatto solver, adjoint sensitivities, output interpolated within the step, explicit solvers with constraints - first on the symbolic system as test and evaluation, then a cleanup of the C++ solvers and the adaptive solvers in C++. Design docs/dev/design/advancedSolvers.md; no issues before the evaluation is decided, except RG20.5. Requested as group 16, a number that revision2026b has taken, as 15, 17 and 18 (step numbers are permanent).

Decided (maintainer 2026-11-04, design section 4): the error estimate of TrapezoidalGGL by the acceleration difference; the 3-stage Lobatto solver named Lobatto4, the variant (IIIC with the constraints at all stages, or IIIA with GGL) chosen in RG20.2.2, the error estimate by the embedded trapezoidal solution and the classical IIIA/IIIC estimate, Radau3 kept in mind as another tableau (no step); the tolerances timeIntegration.absoluteTolerance and relativeTolerance for the explicit and the implicit adaptive solvers; Python first everywhere - the new solvers in a module of their own -, C++ when settled; the explicit elimination as in C++; a common step size controller as step RG20.8.

### **CLOSED_RG20.1** TrapezoidalGGL with an adaptive step size in the symbolic solvers

Design section 3.1. #3242, #3243. CLOSED 2026-11-04: SolveTrapezoidalGGL with constant and controlled steps in exudyn.symbolics.advancedSolvers.

#### **CLOSED_RG20.1.1** Analyze the trapezoidal rule with GGL stabilization, its error estimate and step control

#### **CLOSED_RG20.1.2** TrapezoidalGGL with constant steps in the symbolic solvers, against generalized-alpha

CLOSED 2026-11-04 (#3242): SolveTrapezoidalGGL in the new module exudyn.symbolics.advancedSolvers - v1 = v0 + h/2 (a0 + a1), q1 = q0 + h/2 (v0 + v1) - C_q^T mu, the equations of motion, c = 0 and C_q v + c_t = 0, Newton for a1, y1, z1, mu with the exact Jacobian of the system and (C_q v)_q, C_q from the reaction forces; order 2 in q, v, a and z on a pendulum and a slider crank (against mbs.SolveDynamic), converging to SolveGeneralizedAlpha; the constraints to 1e-12 on both levels. symbolicTrapezoidalGGLTest.py. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg20-1-2)

#### **CLOSED_RG20.1.3** The error estimate and the adaptive step of TrapezoidalGGL, measured against a fine reference

CLOSED 2026-11-04 (#3242): with automaticStepSize the local error h^2/12 (a1 - a0) of the positions, in the norm and the rule of the explicit solvers (absoluteTolerance, relativeTolerance, stepSizeSafety, stepSizeMaxIncrease, order 3); 100 times smaller tolerances give 4.6 times the steps and a 24 times smaller error. Measured: at an equal number of steps the global error is 1 to 10 times that of constant steps, as the estimate does not see the velocity error, and the second difference of the accelerations, which would, is spoiled by their undamped odd-even oscillation; 5-20 % rejected steps of the I controller (RG20.8). [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg20-1-2)

The velocity error by the second difference of the mean accelerations (v1 - v0)/h of three steps, which do not alternate as the end accelerations do. symbolicTrapezoidalGGLStepSizeTest.py. Design section 3.2. #3244, #3246.

#### **CLOSED_RG20.2.1** Analyze the Lobatto methods for constrained systems, the error estimate and the name

CLOSED 2026-11-04: design section 3.2 - Lobatto IIIA with 3 stages, the IIIA-IIIB pair for index 3, the error estimate, the name. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg6-20-rg19-rg20)

#### **CLOSED_RG20.2.2** Lobatto4 with constant steps: Lobatto IIIC with the constraints at all stages against IIIA with GGL, the variant chosen by order and stability on a pendulum and a slider crank

CLOSED 2026-11-04 (#3244): SolveLobatto4 of exudyn.symbolics.advancedSolvers with constant steps, one stage solver for stiffly accurate tableaus with or without GGL at the implicit stages (its 2-stage IIIA-GGL is SolveTrapezoidalGGL to 5e-16). Pendulum: IIIA-GGL and IIIC-GGL of order 4 in q and v, 2 in a and z (IIIA-GGL with 25 times smaller errors of a); IIIC with the constraints at all stages of order 4 in q, 2 in v, 1 in a, and the velocity constraint drifts (2e-3); IIIA without GGL does not converge in a and z. Slider crank: IIIC-GGL of order 4.2. A fast mode of h omega = 70: IIIC-GGL damps it, IIIA-GGL does not converge (at h omega = 14 it keeps the energy to 1e-3). Chosen: IIIC-GGL as the default, IIIA-GGL and IIIC as variants. symbolicLobatto4Test.py. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg20-2-2)

#### **CLOSED_RG20.2.3** The error estimate of Lobatto4 (the embedded trapezoidal solution, the classical IIIA/IIIC estimate) and its adaptive step

CLOSED 2026-11-04 (#3246): the automatic step size of SolveLobatto4 by the embedded trapezoidal solution of the first and the last stage (maintainer: first), with the PI controller of RG20.8.1, order 3 of the local error. It works and rejects little, but it estimates the order-2 error: Lobatto4 takes about the steps of the trapezoidal rule with errors far below the tolerance (slider crank, 1e-6: 68 steps, 4e-10; ball, 1e-6: 433 steps, 3e-8, TrapezoidalGGL 419 steps, 9e-5). RG20.2.5 for an estimate of order 4. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg20-2-3)

#### **CLOSED_RG20.2.4** Lobatto4 with the constraints at the end of the step, without GGL

CLOSED 2026-11-04 (#3246): maintainer: one of the 3-stage Lobatto methods worked in HOTINT with index-3 constraints without GGL, possibly with the constraints at the end of the step only. With one multiplier constant over the step and c = 0 at the end: IIIA, IIIB, IIIC and the pair IIIA-IIIB of order 4 in q, 2 in v, the velocity constraint drifting. With a multiplier per stage after the start (the start that of the previous end) and c = 0 and C_q v + c_t = 0 at the end: IIIA, IIIC and IIIA-IIIB of order 4 in q and v and 2 in a and z. In SolveLobatto4 as 'IIIA-end' (the default) and 'IIIC-end'; half the time of the GGL variants on the slider crank; plain IIIC with the constraints at all stages dropped. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg20-2-3)

#### **CLOSED_RG20.2.5** An error estimate of the order of the method for the Runge-Kutta methods, from the stages of two steps

CLOSED 2026-11-04 (#3250): maintainer: step doubling only as the reference, the formulas evaluated without a solve. The trapezoidal evaluations of one step and two substeps extrapolate to Simpson, i.e. q1 itself - no estimate of order 4 from one step. The quadrature of the method against the polynomial through the stage velocities of the previous and this step (Lobatto: the fourth difference, Simpson's error): 0.3-0.9 times the true local error at all step sizes; the accelerations only without constraints; 4 to 23 times fewer steps. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg20-2-5)

#### **CLOSED_RG20.2.6** The three methods LobattoIIIA4, LobattoIIIC4-GGL and Radau3 of SolveImplicitRungeKutta; the formulas of the error estimates

CLOSED 2026-11-04 (#3247): maintainer: keep IIIA-end as LobattoIIIA4 - the simplest form that solves index 3 without tricks -, IIIC-GGL as LobattoIIIC4-GGL, and Radau3, three methods of two implicit stages; IIIC-end and IIIA-GGL dropped. SolveImplicitRungeKutta(system, settings, method) replaces SolveLobatto4. Radau IIA (c = 1/3, 1) with the constraints at the end: order 3 in q and v, 2 in a and z (with GGL the same); it damps a fast mode of h omega = 70 and the slow motion slightly. The design file with the formulas of the error estimates: the trapezoidal solutions of one step and of two substeps from the stages are the same second difference (Simpson = (4 T2 - T1)/3), no order-4 estimate from the three stages of a step; the candidates for RG20.2.5. #### **CLOSED_RG20.7.4** The order of the implicit Runge-Kutta solvers on the spatial slider crank: the initial accelerations, the stages of Lie group nodes

CLOSED 2026-11-06 (#3289, #3290; maintainer 2026-11-06: the open steps): two causes. The initial accelerations were wrong with Euler parameters (C_q v without its part along the parameters) and Lie group nodes ((C_q v)_q by adding to the rotation vector) - (C_q v)_q v is now a central difference of fourth order along the motion; LobattoIIIA4 uses them in its first stage. The stages of Lie group nodes took the increment of the other coordinates - now Runge-Kutta-Munthe-Kaas, Delta_i = h sum_j A_ij TExpSO3Inv(Delta_j) omega_j by a fixed-point iteration. Orders now 4, 4, 3, 2 with both node types; initialAccelerationsTest.py with a rigid body. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg20-7-4)

#### **CLOSED_RG20.9.9** The Newton floor of LobattoIIIA4 and Radau3 at small steps

CLOSED 2026-11-06 (#3307; maintainer 2026-11-06): the Newton iteration followed the rounding of the position constraints at the end of the step, which the rows of 1/h^2 turn into increments of the accelerations of eps |q| / h^2; with the Jacobian of the end of the step at all stages they left a residual of the stage equations growing as 1/h. A position constraint residual below 16 eps max|q_ref + q| counts as zero; LobattoIIIA4 and Radau3 run on nPendulum and rigidBodyJoints with the step 1e-5 and the Newton tolerance 1e-12 (implicitRungeKuttaSmallStepTest.py), on sliderCrank3D with 1.6e-5 and on FFRFreducedOrder with 7.8e-6. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg20-9-9)

#### **CLOSED_RG20.9.10** Stage Jacobian entries by the first Euler parameter with the opposite sign

CLOSED 2026-11-06 (#3308; maintainer 2026-11-06): no defect. The Jacobian of an item by Euler parameters is the derivative along the rotations, -A p~ G; its component along the parameters themselves is zero (G theta = 0), while the rotation matrix, quadratic in the parameters, changes along them. The finite differences of the check contain that component, which at small angles makes the entries of the first parameter the negatives of the Jacobian; the tangential parts agree to rounding. The constraint of the Euler parameters fixes that direction, and the reaction forces use the same G. A comment in CheckJacobian says so. [log](exudynRevisionLog2026c.md#rg20-9-10)

## **CLOSED_RG21** Path planning and navigation of robots

Maintainer 2026-11-06: path and trajectory planning and navigation for mobile and serial robots in Python, on the C++ kinematics, in a new file of exudyn/robotics that can hold SLAM later; no MoveIt; collision shapes from the contact primitives of RG9.10 (sphere, cylinder, triangle, convex) with a distance output, optionally from the URDF; joint limits respected; 1 to 3 robust algorithms per kind of robot (a grid-based one, an RRT variant); the result as a Trajectory. Design docs/dev/design/robotPlanning.md; no issues before the evaluation is decided.

### **CLOSED_RG21.1** Evaluate path planning and navigation, propose the design

Maintainer 2026-11-06: start with the design file and the evaluation. docs/dev/design/robotPlanning.md: the name navigation.py proposed (collision.py for the shapes), grid A* for mobile robots, RRT-Connect with shortcut smoothing for serial robots and in SE(2), the collision check in C++ as cUtilities.CollisionModel on the distance kernels of RG9.12, collision shapes from the URDF, Trajectory.FromWaypoints with blending, pure pursuit for differential drives; seven decisions for the maintainer and five further steps proposed in its section 6.

CLOSED 2026-11-07 (#3316): decided by the maintainer, design section 5 - navigation.py, the collision shapes next to RobotLink and Robot, the check in C++ only, the common URDF collision geometries, examples with the UR5, a mecanum-wheeled and a differential-drive robot.

## **OPEN_RG22** Nonlinear finite elements: tetrahedra

Maintainer 2026-11-12: nonlinear tetrahedra in Exudyn, so that the reduced-order and FFRF bodies can be compared with fully nonlinear solutions; #3451 waits on them. Also the very large sparse systems for which PARDISO is expected to pay (RG5.9).

### **OPEN_RG22.1** Geometrically nonlinear 4- and 10-node tetrahedra

#3453. Linear and quadratic tetrahedra on NodePoint coordinates, total Lagrangian, with the mass matrix, the internal forces and an analytic Jacobian, for meshes imported through FEMinterface (NGsolve, Abaqus).

### **OPEN_RG22.2** MarkerSuperElementRigid: the tangent operator in the rotation Jacobian without useAlternativeApproach

#3451, found in #3450 (RG5.4.2.1): the Jacobian of the rotation leaves out TexpSO3 with useAlternativeApproach = False, while the angular velocity applies it. Maintainer 2026-11-12: analyzed when the nonlinear tetrahedra exist (RG22.1), by comparing the fully nonlinear model with the reduced-order one; moved from RG5.4.5.
